For as every good thing is of the fear and love of God, so every evil is of the love and fear of the world. These being shut, that is, taken away, pray.

99D(CHRYSOST.) What then, sayest thou? ought one not to pray in the church? And very much indeed, but with this will, that thou look upon that one alone who has the power of granting what thou shalt have asked. For God everywhere beholds with what regard thou doest anything.

The Father. Whom thou hast within by faith; whence: With me is prayer to the God of my life (Psal. XLI).

VERS. 7.-- But praying. As it is of hypocrites to be shown and to please men, so it is of the Gentiles to use much speaking, from whom also it began. For these give more pains to exercising the tongue than to cleansing the mind: so that they transfer this kind of trifling office even to the bending of God by prayer, thinking that as an orator bends a judge, so they too can bend God.

100A(CHRYSOST.) If we must pray in few words, why did Luke set forth a parable that we must always pray upon the mountain, etc., as far as If indeed both Christ and Paul commanded that prayers be made short, but frequent, at short intervals.

(CASS.) They (the Fathers) judge it more useful that prayers be short indeed, but very frequent, etc., as far as let us avoid by a concise brevity.

For they think. Thinking that God is soothed by persuasive words. Or that if he is absent he is called: and that what he does not know he is taught by the windings of words.

VERS. 8.-- Be not therefore like to them. For with God the business is not to be done with words, but with things, with the intention of the heart, with a simple affection. Which things when we say, we do not teach him, but we ourselves remember at the time of praying. 100BFor the Father knoweth. Why then do we speak to one who knows? Not that we may make a declaration of our will, but that we may stir up the service of a devout affection.

VERS. 9. Our Father. (BEDA.) Among the other saving and divine admonitions with which he takes counsel for believers, he set forth a form of praying, etc., as far as and these are the true adorers, who as Sons by grace adore the Father in spirit and in truth.

No prayer is more spiritual than that which proceeded from the mouth of the Son, who is Truth. Our Father. Whom we serve not by fear, but by love of the inheritance. While he is called Father, let us think of adoption, that we may repay to the Father a dutiful affection. While it is said our, he is admonished of brotherhood, since the adoption is common to all. Let no one say "mine," which belongs properly to the Son, 100Cto whom he is Father by nature. Whence elsewhere. I go to my Father (Joan. VII), specially by nature: and yours, joined by grace. Our Father is said, which is common to all, let no one say "mine": which belongs to Christ alone, who is Son by nature; to others is granted the common grace of adoption.

(RAB.) The noble and the rich man is admonished not to be proud, etc., as far as What will he deny to sons, who has already given that he is Father?

(AUG.) Nowhere is a precept found for the people of Israel, that it should say: Our Father, or should pray, etc., as far as if they did not stray from his commandments. As is that: I have begotten sons and exalted them (Isa. I) [Vulg. Is 1:2 Filios enutrivi, et exaltavi, "I have brought up children, and exalted them"], etc

In the heavens. The spiritual ones, that we may learn to hasten to the spirituality in which 100Dhe dwells, and may seek heavenly things from him.

Hallowed be thy name. The distinction of these seven petitions is to be observed. For since our life is carried on in time and eternal life is hoped for, the former are, etc., as far as for liberation makes free men, that is, sons, so that we may say in the spirit of adoption, Abba, Father.

Amen, signifies that in all these petitions what is asked is granted by the Lord without doubt, if we do not neglect to keep the compact of the last condition. Whence he adds: If you shall forgive, etc. Hallowed be, etc. Here we pray for the spirit of wisdom, that we may not, first, in anything depart from the sanctification of the name: but that the name of the Father may be shown in the sons by manners and by life.

101AThe name of the Father is in us, who are called sons: which we ask to be hallowed. Not that he himself may be holier, but that he may work in us an increase of his sanctification: for the sanctification of the sons is attributed to his name. So he is said to be hallowed as he is glorified and magnified in us: Hallowed be, that is, so as it is holy, let it be held by men: and so let God become known to them, that they may not think anything holier.

VERS.10.-- Thy kingdom come. Here the spirit of understanding is asked for, by which the heart is cleansed that God may be seen. The hardness and power of the devil is shut out, and in the future more perfectly, when he shall have made void all principality and power, and the kingdom shall be delivered to God the Father. It fittingly follows, that after the adoption of sons we should ask for the kingdom which is due to sons.

101BThe kingdom of God always is: but let it come, that is, let it be made manifest to men by whom, as light by the blind, it is not seen, which no one will be able to be ignorant of, when the Son shall have come to judge. In which the saints also shall reign, as it is said, Receive the kingdom which is prepared for you (Matth. XXV) [Vulg. Mt 25:34 possidete paratum vobis regnum, "possess the kingdom prepared for you"], etc.

By the spirit of understanding the future kingdom is declared to those entering in, and cleanness of heart is bestowed, through which God, reigning there, shall be seen as he is, where by the spirit of wisdom the seventh sanctification of his name shall be completed. In the perfection of the number six the coming of the kingdom is asked for, that we may all run into the perfect man: then, for those resting in him, may he himself be hallowed in us while we are hallowed in him. His rest we rightly ask to be hallowed in us, that being hallowed in him we may rest perpetually, in which sanctification the peace 101Cof blessedness is made firm and full wisdom is given.

Thy will be done. Here the spirit of counsel is asked for, by which the will of God is sought out, that it may be fulfilled by us.

The will of God on earth as in heaven is governed by the spirit of counsel. We too have need of the spirit of counsel: that the will of God may be directed in us, that we may be able to be merciful in deed. The will of God is always done: but we pray that the liberty of our choice may be joined by grace to his will, which is our sanctification and life everlasting. The will of God is the keeping of his commandments.

As in heaven. As in the angels, so in men. Or as in the just, so in sinners converted. Or 101Das in Christ, so in the Church. Or as in mind I serve the law of God, so also in the flesh.

According to the spirit we are heaven, according to the flesh we are earth. We pray therefore that as in heaven, that is, in the spirit, so on earth the will may be done: that flesh and spirit may be a spiritual man, of whom it is said: My heart and my flesh have rejoiced in the living God. Nor is it only prayed that we may will good things, but that the will may be done in us, that we may live to thee, not to ourselves. These three are had more perfectly in the future, but here meanwhile we pray in part, that afterwards they may be had more perfectly. The four following are the safeguards of the present life.

VERS. 11.-- Bread. The hunger of justice, that it may attain to fullness, is propped up by the spirit of fortitude. Therefore 102Awe pray: Give us this day our daily bread: without which we should die of hunger.

Bread is the body of Christ, or the word of God, or God himself, of whom we daily have need, or that which is above all substances. This is the living bread which came down from heaven (Joan. VI) [Vulg. Jo 6:51 Ego sum panis vivus, qui de caelo descendi, "I am the living bread which came down from heaven"], the sublimity of whose nature is above all substances.

Our. Because Christ has been made ours, and God the Father with Christ has given us all things. Therefore we pray that what is ours by gift may daily be given by effect.

Supersubstantial. Or daily. Because they that eat me shall yet hunger (Eccl. XLIV). Here daily, in the fatherland eternal. This bread ought continually to refresh us inwardly by faith and charity: because without it 102Bno one can live. Or spiritual precepts are called bread, which must always be practised or kept.

VERS. 12.-- And forgive, etc. After food he asks pardon of sins, that he who is fed by God may live in God, and may come to eternal life.

When we say: Forgive us our debts: we ask for the spirit of knowledge, by which we understand our offences, which without the spirit of knowledge are not understood. For knowledge belongs to the use of temporal things, which is a virtue in avoiding evils and seeking goods, that we may bewail our own contagions and those of others, and may desire good things.

As. Behold with what caution we are bound, in which also he bends us mutually to mercy, and promises pardon to those who sin against him, and promises pardon to us and through 102Cus to others.

We ought not to deny pardon of a fault; whence: When you pray, forgive if you have anything against any man: for otherwise your Father will not forgive you your sins. [Vulg. Mc 11:25 dimittite si quis habetis adversus aliquem : ut et Pater vester, qui in caelis est, dimittat vobis peccata vestra, "forgive if you have anything against any man: that your Father also, who is in heaven, may forgive you your sins"]

It is granted to demand money back from someone, but we never justly deny pardon to one asking it for the debts of sins.

VERS. 13.-- And lead us not. But enlighten us with the spirit of knowledge, that we may know by what evils we are entangled, and we ask that our tears may profit: Forgive us our debts. And lead us not, etc. Not that he himself leads in, but because he deserts and permits us to go. Nor indeed does he pray not to be tempted, but not to be overcome even a little. For temptation is necessary for the crown. And lead us not, etc., deserting us 102Din temptation, do not suffer us to be overcome: even though thou suffer us to be tempted, without which no one is proved.

But deliver us from evil. This one petition contains as much as all the foregoing.

That by the spirit of the fear of God all hurtful things within and without may be driven away: and that under poverty we may live blessedly. But deliver us. Into that which is not yet, suffer us not to be led, and from that into which we have been led, deliver us: which being done, nothing is to be feared, nothing remains to be prayed for.

From evil, from all evil, visible and invisible. This deliverance is daily necessary, since without evil of soul and body there is no living, but it shall be completed in the end, when we shall be immortal and impassible. From the lowest to the highest the ascent is thus made by degrees. From evil, through the spirit of fear, 103Awe are delivered: thence being delivered we desire to be poor in spirit and in goods, while we say, Lead us not into temptation. We ask for the spirit of piety, that the inward man may grow gentle and become meek, lest he be moved by temptation.

VERS. 14.-- For if you shall forgive. He has imposed a form of how we would be judged. Whence for those who pardon there answers before God a confidence of obtaining: but those who exact, the penalty of vengeance or of damnation bites again.

VERS. 15.-- But if you shall not forgive. No excuse is left, because everyone shall be judged in the future according to this sentence. From this it is plain that everyone is condemned in his own unbelief.

VERS. 16.-- When you fast. After he taught how 103Bone must pray, he teaches concerning fasting, which again pertains to cleanness of heart, lest it be not simple toward God, while it is carried away to outward things. Be not made. He does not forbid the virtue, but the feigned appearance of virtue.

It is good to be sorrowful by fasting for sins unto repentance, and to humble our soul; whence: A contrite and humbled heart, O God, thou wilt not despise (Psal. L). But the sadness that is for praise, he forbids. Whence he does not say simply: Do not fast, but adds: like the hypocrites, sad.

They drive beyond bounds. That is, they draw them beyond the bounds of the human condition. As there is boasting from the sheen of garments: so from excessive squalor and leanness. To demolish is as it were to destroy: and to reduce as it were to nothing the comeliness of the countenance. Their faces. With mournful filth in 103Cwhich also there is boasting: and the more do they deceive under the appearance of religion.

Note that sheep ought not to lay aside their own skins, but to be clothed, if sometimes wolves cover themselves with them.

VERS. 17.-- Anoint thy head. Refresh thy mind with spiritual gladness on the days of abstinence: that the odour of love may be fragrant in works of mercy. Wash thy conscience with tears: that it may be clean before God. For the work of mercy does not suffice without a clean conscience: nor this without that. It was the custom among the Palestinians to anoint the head at feasts: therefore anoint thy head, that is, show thyself festive and cheerful: and refresh thy mind with spiritual oil: and seek not praise outwardly for thy fasting. But because thou art thus turned away from the world, rejoice before God: and so shalt thou cleanse thy face, that is, thy heart, from filth: which being removed, 103Dit will be permitted to see God. Anoint thy head. Behold, not yet all things in the New Testament according to the letter. For they would be ridiculous who were smeared with oil in fasting: but of the spirit of his love, in whose sufferings we ought to share, ourselves being to be mortified, the mind ought to be anointed, and not with the oil of flattery: otherwise fastings are vain.

Christ was made an ointment for us: which being poured out through the passion of his death filled his Church with his odour. Therefore we ought not to fast for another's sake: but being poured over with his spirit, mortifying the flesh by abstinence, let us share in his passion. Let us then be anointed with him: that the works of mercy may be heaped up: and charity be poured abroad within: because without him the cup of our fasting is not made drunk with the affection of piety.

104AHe fasts to God who mortifies himself for love of him and bestows on another what he withdraws from himself.

VERS. 18.-- And thy Father. The Father is within through faith: and rewards deeds done in secret. There therefore must one fast, where he himself may see. And it is necessary that he who fasts should so fast that he may please him whom he carries in his breast.

VERS. 19.-- Lay not up treasures. He commands that earthly things be paid out unto cleanness of heart.

VERS 20.-- But to lay up treasures. [Vulg. Mt 6:20 Thesaurizate, imperative, "lay up treasures"] As if: When you fast or do any good work: lay it up there where corruption cannot come.

In heaven. That is, place all your hope in heavenly things: walking by faith, embracing charity. In heaven, that is, in the spiritual firmament: which does not pass away: 104BHeaven and earth shall pass away: but my words shall not pass away. If therefore the heart is in heaven, it is clean, because heavenly things are clean. But if it is rolled about upon the earth, how is it clean? that is, if it does anything in order to obtain earthly things, how is it clean? For if those things are clean in their own kind, yet the worthier thing grows foul while it is mingled with the lower: as gold with silver, silver with lead.

VERS. 21.-- For where the treasure is. If on earth, the heart is below; if in heavenly things, the heart is fixed in Christ.

VERS. 22.-- The lamp. He pursues cleanness of heart. As the eye directs the members to work, so do intention and the light of faith direct all the virtues. A lamp is a light in an earthen vessel, so is charity in faith. When faith shall cease, 104Ccharity alone will be the light. From the outward, he teaches the office of the inward man, by the intention of the mind and the light of faith all the virtues are illuminated, that they may render the body lightsome.

Likewise this chapter is of itself unto cleanness of heart, or by reason of the former works which appear openly.

If the eye be, etc. If with a pure intention thou doest the good things thou canst, the works are of light, even if it does not so appear to men. If the intention is perverse, whatever is done is depraved, even though it seem right.

The eye is one and simple, namely charity, which now shines in faith as in an earthen vessel.

VERS. 23.-- But if the eye. If the intention is not of faith, the whole inward man is dark. Because 104Dwhat is not of faith is sin. Shall be darksome. Since indeed the intention that follows forms the work. Therefore if the good things which thou doest, because they are done with an evil intention, are darkness, what of those which are of themselves evil, and are done with an evil intention? If then the light. The intention with which we do whatever we do, which is known within us, is called light; but the outcome of the thing is uncertain, as when I give or do something to someone I know not whether it turn out to him for good or for evil: and therefore he calls it darkness. He says therefore: If the intention with which thou doest (which is known to thee) is soiled by an appetite for temporal things: how much more the deed itself, whose outcome is doubtful? because even if it turn out well for another, what thou doest not with a good intention will profit thee nothing: because it is imputed to thee how thou hast done it, not how it has turned out for him.

105AVERS. 24.-- No man can, etc. As if: you will not be able to do good things before the eyes of men, and by these to please God. Not to lay up treasure at once in heaven and on earth, nor to serve both vices and virtues. Whatever thou doest, either of the love of God, or of the servitude of the devil.

Since the kingdom of God and of the devil are at variance among themselves, no one can serve as a soldier in both at once: whence they who are baptized renounce the devil and his works.

For either he will hate the one. There is nothing between. For whatever thou doest, either of the love of God, or of the servitude of the devil.

(AUG.) For the devil is always to be held in hatred: but God, etc., as far as but they despise, overcome by their own concupiscence and by diabolical persuasion.

Or he will sustain the one. (ID.) Not indeed that he loves him: 105Bfor no one loves the devil, but being entangled by his own cupidity, etc., as far as even if he love not him whose handmaid he loves.

He will despise the other. Not indeed that he will hate him: for no one's conscience can hate God, which the same Augustine retracted, saying: I do not see that it ought to have been said. For there are many of whom it is written: The pride of them that hate thee (Psal. III), etc.

And mammon. Mammon, in the Syrian tongue, riches, to serve which is to deny God. He does not say to have: which is lawful to be done so that one may dispense it well, but to serve, which belongs to the covetous. This is said to be the name of a demon who presides over riches, not because they are in his dominion: but because he uses these for deceiving, ensnaring men in the nets of riches.

105CVERS. 25.-- Therefore I say to you, etc. Because you cannot serve God and mammon. Be not solicitous, which is to serve riches. He has enjoined contempt of the world and confidence in things to come throughout the whole sermon, since he commanded both to bear evils and not to avenge, and to love enemies, and to flee human glory. Now he wishes to confirm it through faith in the hope of eternal things, because there are many whom the love of present things and despair of things to come make torpid.

In the sweat of the face bread is got ready, but labour is to be exercised, solicitude is to be removed, which disturbs the mind: while it fears lest either the things possessed perish or the things laboured for do not come to hand.

Your soul. That is, to the animal nature to which these things are necessary. Commit your whole hope to God, because you can acquire or retain nothing, 105Dexcept what he has decreed.

Soul is here put for life, which is sustained by food, as there: He that loveth his soul shall lose it (Joan. XII).

The animal nature is nourished by food: and is here put for life: it lives with the cattle. And he who loses it for Christ's sake shall find it spiritual unto life everlasting.

Is not the soul? etc. Not life for the sake of food, nor the body for the sake of raiment, but the contrary. Therefore let not the mind be solicitous, that it may receive that for whose sake it was created.

As if he should say: He who gave the greater, that is, life and body, will give also the lesser, that is, food and clothing. 106ALet no one doubt in these promises of the truth: Let man be what he ought to be, and forthwith all things are added to him for whose sake they were made.

VERS. 26.-- Behold. This concerning food. The saints are deservedly compared to birds, because they seek heaven, and some are so far removed from the world that they now do nothing on earth, but by contemplation alone already dwell in heaven. Of whom it is said: Who are these that fly as clouds? (Isa. LX), etc.

These examples do not forbid providence, that it should not labour, but solicitude, that your whole confidence may be in God, since even the birds live without care.

And your Father. He heightens and confirms the confidence of sons: because if the goodness of the Father extends itself even to those things, how much more to the sons of adoption?

106BVERS. 27.-- And which of you, etc. But leave to him the care of governing the body, who made it come to this measure.

VERS. 28.-- And for raiment. [Vulg. Mt 6:28 Et de vestimento, singular] Not that this, to labour and to provide, is not fitting, but all solicitude must be cast away, and hope must be had in God. Consider the lilies of the field. He confirms by a sufficiently fitting example the exhortation concerning clothing.

VERS. 29.-- That not even Solomon, etc. What purple of kings, what painting of weaving-women can be compared to flowers? The colour itself is called the covering of the flowers, as we say: a redness has covered that man.

VERS. 30.-- For if the grass of the field. [Vulg. Mt 6:30 Si autem foenum agri, "But if the grass of the field"] If those things which are for the use of time only, and quickly falling, shall be burnt with fire, God adorns them so beautifully.

106C"Tomorrow" is put for the future in the Scriptures, Jacob saying: My justice shall hear me tomorrow (Gen. XXX) [Vulg. Gn 30:33 Respondebitque mihi cras justitia mea, "And my justice shall answer for me tomorrow"].

Is cast into the oven. Other books have: into the fire, or into a heap which has the appearance of an oven. How much more you, namely, will he clothe; and therefore all confidence is to be had in him; but if we are not confident even about these things, how do we hope for things invisible? Very small is the faith which is not certain even about the least things. Therefore he both presses upon us confidence about eternal things, and condemns unbelief. Of little faith. Whosoever doubts in any promise, is of little faith, and the more he doubts, the less he finds those things for which he was solicitous.

VERS. 31.-- Be not therefore solicitous, etc. He more fully inculcates 106Dand turns over what he taught from the beginning of the sermon, that we should live without solicitude. Be not, etc. Here those seem to be reproved who, despising the common food or clothing, seek for themselves foods or garments more sumptuous or more austere than those among whom they live. To be solicitous. Solicitude chokes the word of God, that it may not bear fruit, and makes a man like a Gentile, that is, unbelieving.

VERS. 32.-- For all these things. What more then has he than a Gentile: whose unbelief makes the mind anxious, and wearies it with the cares of this life? The Gentiles. Who have no care about things to come, but it belongs to sons to seek the kingdom. For he knoweth. These things are provided by God, who knows that they are necessary for us on his way. Your Father. Thus he makes sons secure concerning 107Athe goodness and providence of the Father, that is, without care.

VERS. 33.-- Seek therefore. Here he plainly shows that these things are not to be sought as our goods, although they are necessary. But the kingdom of God is to be sought, and in it our end is to be placed, for whose sake we should do all things: for example: Let us eat that we may preach the Gospel, we do not preach the Gospel that we may eat. When he says "first," he signifies that this is later, not in time, but in dignity. For that is to be sought, this is necessary for the sake of that: therefore seek first the kingdom of God, that is, set it before all other things.

And all these things. Because all things are the sons': and therefore all these things shall be added, even to those who do not seek them, from whom if they are withdrawn, it is for a trial; if 107Bthey are given, it is for thanksgiving; because all things work together unto good.

VERS. 34.-- Solicitous. By fear, by grief, and by the anxiety of cares, which disturb the mind.

For the morrow. (AUG.) For the future, concerning present things he grants it: whence the Apostle: Night and day we worked, that we might not burden any man (I Thes. II) [Vulg. 1Thes 2:9 nocte ac die operantes, ne quem vestrum gravaremus, "working night and day, that we might not burden any of you"]. Concerning things to come, which the divine ordering provides for, it is not fitting, etc., as far as because he who gave this precept, to whom angels ministered, in order that this example might be given had purses: whence he might supply the things necessary for their uses, of which Judas was the keeper.

The day. Not that which the Lord made, but that which Job and Jeremias cursed, namely that which the devil found. It sufficeth. And because minds are overwhelmed by present care, the malice of God [Dei; read diei, "of the day"] ought not to be made to sprout.

CAPUT VII.

  • 107C

VERS. 1.-- Nolite judicare. De dubiis, quia de manifestis judicatur, et non de illis de quibus incertum est quo animo fiant: de quibus nemo sine periculo judicat.

Judicium temerarium est quando, quibus commissum non est, judicant, sive sit de manifestis, sive de occultis. Committitur autem judicium vel magistris Ecclesiae vel principibus terrae. Hi ergo si de manifestis judicent, quae scilicet sunt convicta, vel confessa, non temere judicant; si autem de incertis, temere judicant haec judicia prohibet.

VERS. 2.-- In quo enim. Quasi diceret: In qua voluntate benefeceris, in ea liberaberis; et in qua 107Dmale, in eadem punieris. De his enim agitur in quibus vel temere judicantes offendimus, vel recte agentes excusamur; et ideo addit: In qua mensura. Et juste quorum voluntas non habuit finem peccandi, quamvis cum fine peccaverint, non habent finem ultionis; et quibus judicium fuit sine misericordia, sine misericordia judicabuntur.

In qua mensura. Potest in hac mensura in qua aliud mensuratum est, ut triticum, aliud mensurari, ut hordeum: et alia quantitas, ut si unus modius de uno, centum de alio. Ita facit Deus, et non inique ut illi in aeternum.

Et ipsa infamia, vel malum quod tuleris, causa est cur poenam debitam incurras.

108AJuste qui aeternaliter peccato perfrui volunt, aeternam poenam et inveniunt: quorum voluntas non habuit finem peccandi, nec habebit finem torquendi.

VERS. 3.-- Quid autem vides. Ecce moderamen judicii, et praeventus majoribus, alium non judicet de minoribus. Multi enim praeventi majoribus, leviora in fratre magis volunt vituperare et damnare quam emendare. Ut si pleni odio vel invidia, vel malitia, damnent in eo quod delinquit ira; quae est ut festuca, odium, ut trabs. Iratus alicui vult aliquando eum corrigi; qui odit hoc velle nequit: et ideo impossibile dicitur ut festucam fratri demat, qui trabem gestat; prius ergo trabs odii, invidiae, simulationis et totius malitiae rejicienda est, ut sic misericorditer respectu Dei, non nostri, fratrem possimus 108Bincrepare: ut, sive prosit illi correctio, sive non, nos tamen de simplicitate nostri oculi simus securi.

Vix invenitur aliquis ab hoc vitio alienus, quia unusquisque suis favens, aliena facile reprehendit: et aliena gravius tolerando quam sua, sumit auctoritatem arguendi sine exemplo suae emendationis.

Salutem in eo quod temere judicant in oculo habent trabem.

VERS. 4.-- Aut quomodo dicis fratri tuo. Id est, non poteris minus (ut iram) in fratre emendare, donec majus, id est odium, vel invidiam pepuleris de corde tuo.

VERS. 5.-- Hypocrita, ejice. Hi enim odio et livore suscipiunt omnia accusare, et volunt videri consultores sine exemplo emendationis suae. Non 108Cenim est idoneus doctor alii mederi, qui nondum se sanaverit.

VERS. 6.-- Nolite sanctum, etc. Purgato oculo, possunt dispensatores Ecclesiae alios castigare, sed non sanctum canibus dare; quia sicut periculosum est abscondere, ita prodigum est canibus dare. Talibus mystica non sunt aperienda, sed velo tegenda sancta sanctorum: apertiora vero et quasi viliora dici possunt. Nolite. Eadem dicitur sanctum et margarita, id est Evangelium et sacramenta ecclesiastica. Sanctum, quia natura inviolabile, nec debet ab aliquo corrumpi. Margarita, quae in abscondito latet: et de figuris, quasi apertis conchis eruitur, gemma est pretiosa quae non potest corrumpi. Canes vere sunt qui oblatrant, et quod integrum est 108Ddilacerant; porci qui vilipendunt et conculcant: vel canes ad vomitum reversi, porci nondum conversi, sed in luto vitiorum versati.

Et conversi. Ne forte adhuc aliquid audire disrumpant, non margaritas quas conculcant, sed vos indignatione. Non enim facile potest inveniri quod talibus gratum sit.

VERS. 7.-- Petite. His gradibus via, veritas, et vita legitime certantibus, praestatur: Christus est via qua ambulatur, veritas qua invenitur, ostium ad quod pulsatur, ut ad ipsum per ipsum ingrediamur.

(AUG.) Operose quidem quod ista tria inter se differant exponendum putavi, sed longe melius, etc., 109Ausque ad non enim dixit petentibus, et quaerentibus, et pulsantibus.

VERS. 8.-- Omnis enim, etc. Ut praebeat constantiam perseverandi, diligentiam quaerendi, fiduciam impetrandi, addit. Omnis, etc. Supra docuit quid petamus, regnum scilicet. Hic confidentiam, ut nihil haesitantes petamus. Non denegat se petentibus qui sponte obtulit se non petentibus: et quaerentes invenient eum qui non quaerentibus dedit ut invenirent, et pulsantibus aperiet, qui clamat: Ecce ego sto ad ostium et pulso (Apoc. III).

VERS. 9.-- Aut quis. Explanat quid petendum sit, et ex humana circa filios benevolentia diligentiam Patris circa nos commendat. Petentibus Christum, recta fide non datur lapis offensionis nec duritia 109Bcordis. Ita si petieritis patrem vestrum charitatem, sine qua nil sunt caetera, ut sine pane inops est mensa cui contraria est cordis duritia.

VERS. 10.-- Si piscem. Per piscem, fides quae sub fluentis Spiritus sancti nutritur.

Piscem dicit invisibilium fidem: vel propter aquam baptismi, vel quia de invisibilibus locis capitur, vel quia fluctibus hujus mundi non frangitur. In Evangelio Lucae sequitur comparatio ovi et scorpionis. In ovo et spes accipitur, quia in eo nondum est foetus persecutus; sed fovendo speratur. In scorpione, cujus aculeus retro timendus est, accipitur contrarium spei, scilicet retro aspicere, cum spes se debeat in anteriora extendere.

VERS. 11.-- Si ergo vos. Quandoquidem patres 109Cbona tribuunt filiis, ergo multo magis Pater noster, qui bonus est incomparabiliter, bona dabit petentibus se filiis. Nostis bona. Secundum sensum eorum bona qui ea pro bonis habent, quanquam et in rerum natura bona sunt, et in vitam istam pertinentia, quae malus non de suo dat. Domini enim est terra (Psal. XXIII), etc. Vel apostoli mali dicuntur, quantum ad se respectu Dei qui solus bonus, ex quo omnes boni. Dabit bona. Id est Spiritum sanctum. Vel bona scilicet aeterna, quae non dantur nisi filiis. Petentibus se. Omnium bonorum spiritualium distributorem ut opera charitatis impleantur.

VERS. 12.-- Omnia ergo, etc. Quia amare amantem naturae est; non amantem vero cogi beneficiis ad amorem, perfectionis Christi est. Cum nos priores, 109Dut nobis fieri velimus aliis facere juberet, mox eumdem sensum astruit dicens: Omnia ergo quaecunque, etc., id est, bona quae vultis accipere ab aliis, eadem facite illis ut charitas proximi impleatur, quae sine dilectione recte non potest perfici. Et ideo addit: Haec est lex et prophetae, etc.; quia nec Deus sine proximo, nec proximus diligitur sine Deo, quia non ex odio vel ex invidia judicandus est proximus: ergo qua charitate velletis moneri, facite aliis. Ut faciant vobis homines. Quia addit, homines, dicit tantum de dilectione proximi. Unde nec subdit, tota lex, relinquit vero de dilectione Dei. Quod si dixisset: Omnia quaecunque vultis fieri vobis, facite aliis: utrumque complexus esset, in quibus 110Atota lex, propter salutem hominum scriptum est in prophetis et lege.

VERS. 13.-- Intrate. Etsi difficile sit ut aliis facias quod tibi vis fieri, tamen sic faciendum est ut intremus. Per angustam. Angusta quaeritur, nec ab omnibus invenitur. Nec omnes qui inveniunt per eam ingrediuntur, quia capti saeculo retroeunt. Et spatiosa. Hanc etsi non quaerant, omnes tamen inveniunt, quia in ea nati. Voluptas, ut meretrix importuna, suis oblectamentis omnes invitat: et ideo multi intrant per eam.

VERS. 14.-- Quam angusta. Satis angustum est omnia praetermittere, unum solum diligere, unum quaerere, ad unum quotidie pulsare, prospera non ambire, adversa non timere. Et pauci. Si pauci inveniunt, 110Bpauciores per eam intrare contendunt. Nemo intrat sine labore, et sine animi puritate.

VERS. 15.-- Attendite. Cum dixisset arctam viam a paucis inveniri, ne forte sui implicarentur erroribus pseudo-prophetarum, adjecit: Attendite: Attendite. Quod supra latam portam dixit, falsos prophetas apertius dicit. Attendite. Licet hoc de omnibus qui aliud habitu et sermone, aliud opere ostendunt, possit accipi; tamen specialiter de haereticis qui quadam pietatis veste tecti, venenato animo et intentione nocendi lupi sunt rapaces, vel exterius si copia datur persequendo, vel interius corrumpendo.

Ad vos in vestimentis ovium. In conspectu hominum similes ministris justitiae dum jejunant, orant, eleemosynas dant: sed non sunt eorum fructus, quia 110Cpro vitio eis reputatur.

A fructibus eorum. (HIER.) Non a veste, sed ab operibus quod de manifestis, etc., usque ad sed non debent oves pelles suas deponere, etsi aliquando eis lupi se contegant.

VERS. 16.-- Nunquid. Allegorice. Spinae et tribuli sunt haeretici a quibus nullus sapientum sanctitatem vel veritatem poterit invenire: sed conscindunt et cruentant approximantes. Vel spinae et tribuli, curae sunt saeculi, punctionesque vitiorum. Uva et ficus, dulcedo novae conversationis quam Christus in nobis esuriit, et fervor dilectionis, qui laetificat cor hominis. Non ergo de spinis et tribulis uva et ficus: quia mens adhuc veteris hominis consuetudine pressa, potest simulare non ferre fructus 110Dnovi hominis. Quod si aliquando dicta vel facta malorum prosunt bonis, non hoc faciunt mali, sed fit de illis consilio Dei: quia non eorum fructus est, sed vitis quae super spinas aliquando exuberat. Sed carpendus est botrus, spina cavenda. Vitis Christus. Sui, palmites qui ferunt uvas, id est opera spiritus quae inebriant mentem.

VERS. 17.-- Sic omnis arbor. Id est secundum praedictam impossibilitatem: quia de mala radice non potest fructus suavitatis erumpere. Exspectandi sunt boni fructus de bona arbore. Arbor bona vel mala non natura quae in omnibus bona a Deo est creata, sed voluntas bona vel mala. Fructus, opera quae nec bona malae voluntatis possunt esse, nec 111Amala bonae voluntatis. Fructus bonae arboris gaudium, pax, patientia, longanimitas, etc.; quos fructus non nisi bona voluntas parturit. Sic econtrario malos mala intentio nutrit.

VERS. 18.-- Non potest. Non ait: Non potest mala arbor bona fieri, vel bona mala; quia Paulus de persecutore factus apostolus, et anima manente integra natura, affectu voluntatis corrumpitur, vel in melius mutatur.

Voluntas uniuscujusque arbor bona vel mala. Et sicut arbor radicatur in terra, sic voluntas in anima.

Fructus malae arboris sunt opera carnis, scilicet fornicatio immunda, luxuria.

VERS. 19.-- Omnis arbor. Hac sententia hypocritas 111Bet pseudoprophetas damnat, et quod supra docuit de cordis munditia, hoc exemplo confirmat, quod nemo de mala intentione fructum bonum percipit, nec de bona nisi bonum metit.

VERS. 20.-- Igitur ex fructibus. Quasi dicat: A falsis prophetis attendite, et ex fructibus eos cognoscite.

VERS. 21.-- Non omnis qui dicit. Sicut cavendi sunt qui habent speciem bonae vitae propter dogma nequitiae, sic et hic econtrario qui, cum integrae fidei et doctrinae sint, turpiter vivunt. Utrumque enim necesse est, ut et opus sermone et sermo operibus comprobetur.

Qui dicit: Domine, Domine. Iter regni Dei est obedientia, non nominis nuncupatio. Non est hoc illud 111Cvere et proprie dicere ubi prolatio discordat a voluntate. De quo Apostolus ait: Nemo potest dicere: Dominus Jesus, nisi in Spiritu sancto (I Cor. XII).

VERS. 22.-- Multi dicent. Maxime autem cavendum est ab his qui propter nomen Christi etiam miracula habent, quae cum propter infideles Dominus fecerit, monuit tamen ne a talibus decipiamur: putantes ibi esse invisibilem sapientiam, ubi est visibile miraculum; unde adjungit: Multi dicent. Quam mundo ergo et simplici oculo opus est, ut inveniatur via sapientiae inter tot deceptiones.

VERS. 23.-- Nunquam novi vos. Non novit lux tenebras, id est, non aspicit, quia si aspiceret, tenebrae non essent. Qui operamini iniquitatem. Non qui operati estis, ne tollat poenitentiam, sed qui in judicio; 111Dlicet non habeatis facultatem peccandi, tamen habetis affectum.

VERS. 24.-- Omnis ergo. Quasi dicat: Omnia praedicta bene tenenda sunt, quia qui tenet assimilabitur Christo; qui non tenet, diabolo. Qui autem, quae in monte habita sunt: quod dicit ut ostendat sermonem in monte habitum, omnibus praeceptis quibus Christiana vita informatur, esse perfectum. Assimilabitur. Quia sicut Christus de personis variis unam Ecclesiam construit, erudit, regit in vitam dedicaturus aeternam, sic iste variis virtutibus perpetuam mansionem sibi aedificat, et in futura dedicatione cum Christo laetabitur. Viro. Illi qui dicit: Ut sapiens architectus, fundamentum posui in Christo. 112AQui aedificavit. Ille aedificat in Christo, qui quod audit ab illo facit. Supra petram. A qua Petrus nomen accepit pro firmitate fidei, id est, super seipsum aedificavit Ecclesiam.

VERS. 25.-- Et descendit. Tribus modis diabolus impugnat: carnis concupiscentia, exterioribus mundi oblectamentis, vel per se, cum universos ventus impulsionum laxat. Pluvia. Concupiscentia, a qua quisque tentatur, quae sensum rigorem fidei emollit, quae si non proficit, majora flumina eveniunt.

VERS. 26.-- Qui audit verba mea, etc., exterius. Et ideo non habet excusationem, sed est ruina ejus magna: quia monita salutis contempsit. Viro stulto. De quo dicitur: Inimicus homo hoc fecit, cujus opera omnia instabilia sunt, et ruinae patent. Super arenam. 112BQualiacunque sint quae super concupiscentiam vitiorum fundantur, non stabunt.

VERS. 27.-- Et cecidit fuitque ruina ejus magna. Omnis conscientia quae fixa in Deum spe non permanet, in tentationibus non valet subsistere; et tanto amplius agitur, quanto plus in his quae mundi sunt a superioribus disjungitur: et vere omnes mali vel ficte boni, ingruente qualibet tentatione pejores fiunt.

VERS. 28.-- Turbae. Vel aliqui in turba, quia infideles, vel potius omnes de excellentia sapientiae.

CHAPTER VII.

  • 107C

VERS. 1.-- Judge not. Of doubtful matters, since judgment is passed on manifest ones, and not on those of which it is uncertain with what mind they are done: of which no one judges without danger.

Rash judgment is when men judge in matters not committed to them, whether it be of manifest things or of hidden ones. Now judgment is committed either to the masters of the Church or to the princes of the earth. These therefore, if they judge of manifest things, those namely which are proved or confessed, do not judge rashly; but if of uncertain things, they judge rashly: these judgments he forbids.

VERS. 2.-- For with what. As if he were to say: In what will thou hast done well, in that same shalt thou be set free; and in what 107Dill, in that same shalt thou be punished. For the matter here concerns those things in which we either offend by judging rashly, or are excused by acting rightly; and therefore he adds: With what measure. And justly, those whose will had no end of sinning, although they sinned with an end, have no end of vengeance; and those whose judgment was without mercy shall be judged without mercy.

With what measure. It can be that in this same measure, in which one thing has been measured out, wheat for instance, another should be measured, barley for instance: and another quantity too, as if one bushel of the one, a hundred of the other. So God does, and not unjustly, that for them [it be] unto eternity.

And the very infamy, or the evil which thou hast brought, is the cause why thou incurrest the punishment that is due.

108AJustly do those who wish to enjoy sin eternally find also an eternal punishment: whose will had no end of sinning, nor shall it have an end of being tormented.

VERS. 3.-- And why seest thou. Behold the moderation of judgment, and let not one who is himself forestalled by greater faults judge another for lesser ones. For many, forestalled by greater faults, would rather find fault with lighter ones in a brother and condemn them than amend them. As if, being full of hatred or envy or malice, they should condemn in him that wherein anger offends; which anger is as a mote, and hatred as a beam. One who is angry with another sometimes wishes him to be corrected; he who hates cannot wish this: and therefore it is called impossible that he should take away the mote from his brother who carries a beam; first therefore the beam of hatred, of envy, of dissimulation and of all malice must be cast away, so that we may thus mercifully, with regard to God, not to ourselves, rebuke 108Bour brother: so that, whether the correction profit him or not, we at any rate may be secure concerning the singleness of our own eye.

Scarcely is anyone found free from this vice, since each man, favouring his own, easily reprehends what is another's: and, bearing another's faults more heavily than his own, he assumes the authority of reproving without the example of his own amendment.

Salvation, in that they judge rashly they have a beam in the eye.

VERS. 4.-- Or how sayest thou to thy brother. That is, thou wilt not be able to amend the lesser thing (anger, for instance) in thy brother, until thou hast driven the greater, that is hatred, or envy, out of thy own heart.

VERS. 5.-- Thou hypocrite, cast out. For these men take it upon themselves out of hatred and spite to accuse everything, and wish to seem counsellors without the example of their own amendment. For 108Che is no fit teacher to heal another who has not yet healed himself.

VERS. 6.-- Give not that which is holy, etc. The eye being cleansed, the stewards of the Church can chastise others, but not give what is holy to dogs; for as it is dangerous to hide it, so it is prodigal to give it to dogs. To such men mystical things are not to be opened, but the holy of holies is to be covered with a veil: yet things more open, and as it were commoner, may be spoken. Give not. The same thing is called both what is holy and a pearl, that is, the Gospel and the sacraments of the Church. That which is holy, because by nature inviolable, and it ought not to be corrupted by anyone. A pearl, which lies hidden in secret: and out of the figures, as out of opened shells, is drawn a precious gem which cannot be corrupted. Dogs indeed are those who bark against it and tear in pieces 108Dwhat is whole; swine, those who set it at nought and trample it: or dogs are those returned to their vomit, swine those not yet converted, but wallowing in the mire of the vices.

And turning upon you. Lest perchance, hearing yet something further, they rend, not the pearls, which they trample, but you, by their indignation. For it cannot easily be found what would be pleasing to such men.

VERS. 7.-- Ask. By these steps the way, the truth, and the life are granted to those who strive lawfully: Christ is the way by which one walks, the truth by which he is found, the door at which one knocks, that we may enter to him through him.

(AUG.) Laboriously indeed did I think it must be set forth how these three differ among themselves, but far better, etc., 109Aas far as for he did not say, to those asking, and seeking, and knocking.

VERS. 8.-- For every one, etc. That he may furnish constancy in persevering, diligence in seeking, confidence in obtaining, he adds. Every one, etc. Above he taught what we should ask, namely the kingdom. Here, confidence, that we may ask hesitating in nothing. He does not deny himself to those who ask, who freely offered himself to those who did not ask: and those who seek shall find him who granted to those who did not seek that they should find, and to those who knock he will open, who cries: Behold, I stand at the door and knock (Apoc. III).

VERS. 9.-- Or what man. He explains what is to be asked, and from human kindness toward children he commends the Father's care toward us. To those who ask for Christ with right faith there is given no stone of stumbling, nor hardness 109Bof heart. So if you ask your father for charity, without which all else is nothing, as without bread the table is destitute, to which hardness of heart is contrary.

VERS. 10.-- If he ask a fish. By the fish, the faith which is nourished beneath the streams of the Holy Spirit.

By the fish he means faith in things invisible: either because of the water of baptism, or because it is taken out of invisible places, or because it is not broken by the waves of this world. In Luke's Gospel there follows the comparison of the egg and the scorpion. In the egg hope also is understood, because in it the young is not yet brought through; but by cherishing it is hoped for. In the scorpion, whose sting is to be feared behind, is understood the contrary of hope, namely to look backward, whereas hope ought to stretch itself forward.

VERS. 11.-- If you then. Since fathers 109Cbestow good things upon their children, therefore much more will our Father, who is incomparably good, give good things to the children who ask him. You know how to give good things. Good according to the judgment of those who hold them for good, although in the nature of things they too are good, and pertain to this present life, which an evil man does not give of his own. For the earth is the Lord's (Psal. XXIII), etc. Or the apostles are called evil, as regards themselves in comparison with God, who alone is good, from whom all are good. Will give good things. That is, the Holy Spirit. Or good things, namely eternal ones, which are not given save to sons. To them that ask him. The distributor of all spiritual goods, that the works of charity may be fulfilled.

VERS. 12.-- All things therefore, etc. Because to love one who loves is a thing of nature; but that one who does not love should be constrained by benefits to love, is of Christ's perfection. When he had commanded us first, 109Dto do to others what we would wish to be done to us, he at once builds up the same sense, saying: All things therefore whatsoever, etc., that is, the good things which you wish to receive from others, do the same to them, that the charity of the neighbour may be fulfilled, which without love cannot rightly be perfected. And therefore he adds: This is the law and the prophets, etc.; because neither is God loved without the neighbour, nor the neighbour without God, since the neighbour is not to be judged out of hatred or out of envy: therefore with what charity you would wish to be admonished, do to others. That men should do to you. Because he adds "men", he speaks only of the love of the neighbour. Whence neither does he subjoin "the whole law", but leaves aside what belongs to the love of God. But if he had said: All things whatsoever you wish to be done to you, do to others: he would have embraced both, in which 110Ais the whole law, for the salvation of men it is written in the prophets and in the law.

VERS. 13.-- Enter ye. Although it be hard that thou shouldst do to others what thou wishest to be done to thee, yet so it must be done that we may enter. By the narrow gate. The narrow one is sought, and is not found by all. Nor do all who find it enter by it, because, taken captive by the world, they go back. And broad. Although they do not seek this one, yet all find it, because they were born in it. Pleasure, like an importunate harlot, invites all by her delights: and therefore many enter by it.

VERS. 14.-- How narrow. It is narrow enough to pass by all things, to love one alone, to seek one, to knock daily at one, not to court prosperity, not to fear adversity. And few. If few find it, 110Bfewer strive to enter by it. No one enters without labour, and without purity of mind.

VERS. 15.-- Beware. When he had said that the narrow way is found by few, lest perchance his own should be entangled in the errors of the pseudo-prophets, he added: Beware: Beware. What above he called the broad gate, he more openly calls false prophets. Beware. Although this may be understood of all who show one thing in dress and speech and another in deed; yet it is especially of heretics, who, covered with a certain garment of piety, are ravening wolves with a venomous mind and an intent to harm, either outwardly by persecuting, if opportunity be given, or inwardly by corrupting.

To you in the clothing of sheep. In the sight of men they are like ministers of justice while they fast, pray, give alms: but these are not their fruits, because 110Cit is reckoned to them for a vice.

By their fruits. (HIER.) Not by the vesture, but by the works, which is of things manifest, etc., as far as but the sheep ought not to lay aside their fleeces, even if wolves sometimes cover themselves with them.

VERS. 16.-- Do they gather. Allegorically. Thorns and thistles are the heretics, from whom no one of the wise will be able to find holiness or truth: rather they tear and bloody those who come near. Or thorns and thistles are the cares of the world, and the prickings of the vices. The grape and the fig are the sweetness of the new manner of life, for which Christ hungers in us, and the fervour of love, which gladdens the heart of man. Not from thorns and thistles, therefore, come the grape and the fig: because a mind still pressed down by the habit of the old man can feign, not bear, the fruits 110Dof the new man. But if sometimes the words or deeds of evil men profit the good, the evil do not do this, but it comes about from them by the counsel of God: because it is not their fruit, but the vine's, which sometimes runs over upon the thorns. But the cluster is to be plucked, the thorn avoided. Christ is the vine. His own are the branches which bear the grapes, that is, the works of the spirit, which make the mind drunk.

VERS. 17.-- So every tree. That is, according to the aforesaid impossibility: because from an evil root the fruit of sweetness cannot break forth. Good fruits are to be looked for from a good tree. A tree good or evil is not nature, which in all things was created good by God, but the will, good or evil. The fruits are the works, which can neither be good works of an evil will, nor 111Aevil works of a good will. The fruits of a good tree are joy, peace, patience, long-suffering, etc.; which fruits none but a good will brings forth. So on the contrary an evil intention nourishes evil ones.

VERS. 18.-- A good tree cannot. He does not say: An evil tree cannot become good, or a good one evil; for Paul from a persecutor was made an apostle, and, the soul's nature remaining whole, it is corrupted by the affection of the will, or is changed for the better.

The will of each man is a tree good or evil. And as a tree is rooted in the earth, so is the will in the soul.

The fruits of an evil tree are the works of the flesh, namely unclean fornication, luxury.

VERS. 19.-- Every tree. By this sentence he condemns hypocrites 111Band pseudo-prophets, and what he taught above concerning cleanness of heart he confirms by this example, that no one receives good fruit from an evil intention, nor reaps from a good one anything but good.

VERS. 20.-- Therefore by their fruits. As if he should say: Beware of false prophets, and know them by their fruits.

VERS. 21.-- Not every one that saith. As those are to be guarded against who have the appearance of a good life because of a doctrine of wickedness, so here on the contrary are those who, though they be of entire faith and doctrine, live basely. For both are needful, that the work be approved by the word, and the word by the works.

That saith: Lord, Lord. The road of the kingdom of God is obedience, not the calling of a name. This is not that 111Csaying truly and properly, where the utterance is at variance with the will. Of which the Apostle says: No man can say: Lord Jesus, but in the Holy Ghost (I Cor. XII).

VERS. 22.-- Many will say. But most of all are those to be guarded against who, for the name of Christ, have even miracles, and although the Lord wrought these for the sake of unbelievers, yet he warned us not to be deceived by such men: thinking that invisible wisdom is there where there is a visible miracle; whence he adds: Many will say. How clean and single an eye then is needed, that the way of wisdom may be found amid so many deceptions.

VERS. 23.-- I never knew you. The light does not know the darkness, that is, it does not look upon it, since if it looked upon it, it would not be darkness. You that work iniquity. Not "who have worked", lest he take away repentance, but "who work" at the judgment; 111Dalthough you have not the power of sinning, yet you have the desire.

VERS. 24.-- Every one therefore. As if he should say: All the foregoing must be well kept, because he who keeps them shall be likened to Christ; he who keeps them not, to the devil. But he who, which things were delivered on the mount: this he says in order to show that the sermon delivered on the mount is perfect in all the precepts by which the Christian life is formed. Shall be likened. Because, as Christ out of various persons builds up one Church, instructs it, rules it, being about to dedicate it unto eternal life, so this man by various virtues builds for himself a perpetual dwelling, and in the dedication to come will rejoice with Christ. To a man. To him who says: As a wise architect, I have laid the foundation in Christ. 112AWho built. He builds in Christ who does what he hears from him. Upon a rock. From which Peter received his name, for the firmness of his faith, that is, he built the Church upon himself.

VERS. 25.-- And there came down. In three ways the devil assails: by the concupiscence of the flesh, by the outward delights of the world, or by himself, when he looses upon all the wind of his impulsions. The rain. Concupiscence, by which each man is tempted, which softens the sense, the rigour of faith, which if it does not prevail, greater floods come on.

VERS. 26.-- Who heareth these my words, etc., outwardly. And therefore he has no excuse, but great is his ruin: because he despised the warnings of salvation. To a foolish man. Of whom it is said: An enemy hath done this, whose works are all unstable, and lie open to ruin. Upon the sand. 112BOf whatever sort the things be that are founded upon the concupiscence of the vices, they shall not stand.

VERS. 27.-- And it fell, and great was the ruin thereof. Every conscience which does not remain fixed in God by hope has no strength to stand in temptations; and it is driven the further, the more it is severed from the things above in those things which are of the world: and truly all who are evil, or feignedly good, become worse when any temptation presses upon them.

VERS. 28.-- The crowds. Either some in the crowd, because unbelievers, or rather all, [wondered] at the excellence of his wisdom.

CAPUT VIII.

VERS. 1.-- Cum autem descendisset. Post descensionem Domini de monte, etc., usque ad ostendit 112Cpost quantum temporis intervallum. Cum autem descendisset. Secundum Lucam, et in campestri loco sermonem quem jam forsitan supra habuerat ei fecisset. Secutae sunt. Alii propter charitatem, alii propter doctrinam, alii propter admirationem et curationem.

VERS. 2.-- Adorabat eum dicens: Domine. Priusquam peteret, cultum divinitatis ostendit. Si vis, potes me mundare. De voluntate non quasi pietatis incredulus, dubito; sed quasi meae colluvionis conscius non praesumo.

VERS. 3.-- Extendens. Post sermonem et miracula facit, quibus sermo apud auditores confirmatur. Tetigit. Non quia sine tactu sanare posset, sed ut indicet se non subjectum esse legi, sed Dominum legis, 112Dqui non timet contagium. Tetigit. Propter humanitatem, ut nos doceret nullum spernere pro aliqua corporis maculatione. Apparet supra legem (quae leprosum excludit) esse gratiam, quae sanat eum; ut ait: Non veni solvere legem, sed adimplere, etc. Volo. Eo sanavit genere quo fuerat obsecratus, deinde imperat: Mundare. Ecce potestas.

VERS. 4.-- Vide, nemini, etc. Tacere jussit, nec tamen tacere potuit. Non quod aliquid voluit et non potuit, sed dat exemplum, ut sui in magnis quae faciunt latere velint: sed ut prosint aliis, prodantur inviti. Nemini dixeris (Marc. I). Hunc tamen Marcus ubi quod hoc praedicasse dicit quasi Evangelista factus sit. Vade. Mittit ad sacerdotes propter humilitatem, 113Aut sacerdotibus deferat honorem quibus lex jubet mundatos munera offerre, et ut videntes credant ei: vel si non credunt, inexcusabiles sint, et ne legem frangere videretur. Ostende te sacerdoti. Ut sacerdos non legis ordine, sed gratia Dei supra legem curatum esse intelligat. Offer, etc. Quia legem non solvo, quia nondum coepit verum sacrificium. In testimonium. Id est, Domino credant, si impietatis lepra discendat. In testimonium. Spiritualis curationis, offerendum est munus spirituale homo ipse.

VERS. 7.-- Ego veniam et curabo eum. Et respondens centurio ait, etc. Magna humilitas, etc., usque ad ob impotentiam virium corporaliter ire putaretur.

VERS. 8.-- Non sum dignus. Propter conscientiam 113Bvitae gentilis, magis timet gravari praesentia quam juvari, quia et si erat fide praeditus, nondum tamen erat sacramentis imbutus. Sed quod nec infirmitas nostra praesumit gratia dare novit.

VERS. 9.-- Nam et ego, etc. Si ego homo, etc., usque ad ut Cornelius dicitur vir justus et timens Deum (Act. X).

VERS. 11.-- Ab oriente et occidente venient et recumbent. Vel oriens, qui illuminati statim transeunt; occidens, qui passiones usque ad mortem tolerant. Per istas duas partes mundi ex quibus gentes vocandae erant designantur.

VERS. 12.-- Filii autem regni ejicientur. Quia in hoc mundo fruuntur beneficiis Dei; secundum illud: Pluit super justos et injustos. Nunc videntur in 113Cfacie Domini, sed tunc minime. In tenebras exteriores. Quia a Domino foras expellentur, etc., usque ad poenitet quod deliquit pertinaciter. Fletus. Per hoc ostenditur magnitudo tormentorum. Per fletum, tormentum animae; per stridorem dentium, corporis.

VERS. 14.-- Et cum venisset, etc. Quando hoc sit factum non expressit Matthaeus, etc., usque ad narranda recordationi ejus suggerere. Et febricitantem. Febris Graece, fervor Latine dicitur: et si quando haec passio faciat tremere, naturae est humanae.

VERS. 15.-- Et tetigit, etc. Lucas dicit, quod rogaverunt, etc., usque ad ut Delicta quis intelligit: ab occultis meis munda me (Psal. VIII). Et surrexit, etc. 113DNaturale est febricitantibus incipiente sanitate lassescere, etc., usque ad ministrat in spiritu.

VERS. 16.-- Vespere autem facto, etc. Occasus solis, mors est Christi, per quam plures sanavit daemoniacos, quam ante vivens in carne aegrotos.

VERS. 18.-- Videns autem Jesus. Manifestum est alium esse diem, etc., usque ad amaros saeculi fluctus transeamus.

VERS. 19.-- Et accedens. Quod Matthaeus dicit hoc factum quando jussit ire trans fretum: Lucas vero ambulantibus illis in via: non est contrarium: quia via ambulabant ut venirent ad fretum. Magister. Quasi unus sit de pluribus. Et ipse litterator erat, non spiritualis auditor. Magister. Non dixit, 114ADomine, sed magister: unum de pluribus magistris aestimans.

VERS. 20.-- Vulpes foveas habent. Quasi dicat: Quid me propter lucra saeculi, etc., usque ad divinitati Christi non est locus. Filius autem hominis non habet, etc. Cum homines vocent eum Filium Dei, ipse seipsum vocat Filium hominis, humilitatis causa.

VERS. 21.-- Alius autem. Nota hunc vocatum esse a Jesu. Domine. Ille qui ingessit se vocat eum magister, ut elatus Pharisaeus, alter qui receptus est, Dominum vocat humiliter, sicut publicanus.

VERS. 22.-- Mortuos suos. Qui culpa exierunt, ut ipsi cum culpa vivunt. Mortuos sepelire, est peccatores in peccato fovere.

114BVERS. 23.-- Et ascendente. Qui magna fecit in terra, transit ad mare, ut et ibi faciat, et Dominus terrae et maris appareat.

VERS. 24.-- Ecce motus. Non ex se orta est tempestas, sed ejus imperio: ut discipuli magis timeant et rogent: et rogantibus ostendat potentiam. Dormiebat. Dormit in nobis Dominus, id est fides Domini, cum negligentius agimus, et ideo tempestas. Si vero fides resurgit, imperat ventis et fluctibus.

VERS. 25. Suscitaverunt. Primum est Jesus in tribulationibus orationibus excitandus, ut post increpationem surgens imperet ventis et mari, et sit tranquillitas magna.

VERS. 26.-- Modicae, etc. Si novistis potentem 114Cin terra, cur non creditis etiam in mari? Qui parum credit, arguitur: qui nihil, contemnitur. Imperavit, etc. Cui imperatur, imperantem sentit. Nota quod omnis creatura creatorem sentit. Quae apud nos insensibilia, isti sunt sensibilia.

VERS. 27.-- Qualis est hic, etc. (ORIG.) Qualis est iste, major Moyse, etc., usque ad non habent auditum obtemperant.

VERS. 28. In regionem Gerazenorum. Geraza urbs est Arabiae, etc., usque ad a Christo visitata per praedicatores. Duo habentes. Marcus et Lucas dicunt unum, etc., usque ad foeditatem corporum ostendebant. Saevi. Marcus dicit quod nullus jam daemoniacum, etc., usque ad impugnant aliquem transire.

VERS. 29.-- Jesu fili Dei. (HILAR.) Magis suspicati 114Dquam nosse credendi sunt. (AMBR.) Jesu fili Dei. Tantum eis innotuit quantum voluit, etc., usque ad videbant in eo, non credebant. Ante tempus. Sciebant enim sibi futuram in Dei judicio, etc., usque ad fuerunt enim duo millia, ut Marcus ait. Torquere nos. Praesentia Christi tortura est eis; vel, torquentur dum de corporibus ejiciuntur, in quorum tortura delectabantur, sicut praesentia Dei refectio angelorum.

VERS. 31.-- Mitte nos. Nota multo minus eos sua potestate nocere hominibus, qui nec pecoribus possunt. Nisi quis more porci vixerit, diabolus in eum potestatem non accipit, nisi forte ad probandum.

VERS. 32.-- Et ecce magno. Dignior homo bestiis, 115Acum propter salutem duorum hominum duo millia porcorum perierunt. Liberato populo gentium, increduli agunt malos ritus in abditis: caeca et profunda curiositate submersi.

VERS. 33.-- Pastores autem fugerunt. Quia etiam quidam principes impiorum, et, si fugiunt fidem Christi, tamen potentiam ejus mirando nuntiare non cessant.

VERS. 34.-- Exiit obviam. (BED.) Prodeuntes, signant quosdam consuetudine ligatos, mirari quidem et venerari legem Christi, sed nolle pati: unde transire rogant. Et invenerunt hominem (ut Lucas ait) vestitum et sana mente sedere ad pedes Jesu (Luc. VIII). Rogabant eum. Rogant non de superbia de humilitate judicantes se indignos, ut Petrus fecit, 115Bet vidua de Elia. Timebant ne destrueret eos praesentia sanctitatis ejus propter peccata sua, sicut destruxerat porcos.

CHAPTER VIII.

VERS. 1.-- And when he was come down. After the Lord's descent from the mountain, etc., as far as he shows 112Cafter how great an interval of time. And when he was come down. According to Luke, and in a level place, he had delivered to him the discourse which perhaps he had already held above. There followed him. Some for charity's sake, some for doctrine's, some for wonder and for healing.

VERS. 2.-- Adored him, saying: Lord. Before he asked, he showed the worship due to divinity. If thou wilt, thou canst make me clean. Concerning the will I am in doubt, not as one incredulous of his mercy; but, as one conscious of my own foulness, I do not presume.

VERS. 3.-- Stretching forth. After the discourse he works miracles also, by which the discourse is confirmed with the hearers. He touched him. Not because he could heal without touch, but that he might show himself not subject to the law, but the Lord of the law, 112Dwho fears no contagion. He touched him. For humanity's sake, that he might teach us to despise no one for any defilement of the body. It appears that grace, which heals him, is above the law (which shuts out the leper); as he says: I am not come to destroy the law, but to fulfil, etc. I will. He healed in that same manner in which he had been besought, then he commands: Be thou made clean. Behold his power.

VERS. 4.-- See, tell no man, etc. He bade him keep silence, nor yet could he keep silence. Not that he willed something and could not do it, but he gives an example, that his own should wish to lie hidden in the great things they do: yet that they may profit others, they are made known against their will. Tell no man (Marc. I). Yet Mark, where he says that he preached this, [says] that he was as it were made an Evangelist. Go. He sends him to the priests for humility's sake, 113Athat he may render honour to the priests, to whom the law bids the cleansed offer gifts, and that seeing they may believe him: or, if they do not believe, that they may be without excuse, and lest he should seem to break the law. Show thyself to the priest. That the priest may understand that he was healed not by the ordinance of the law, but by the grace of God above the law. Offer, etc. Because I do not destroy the law, because the true sacrifice has not yet begun. For a testimony. That is, let them believe the Lord, if the leprosy of impiety depart. For a testimony. Of the spiritual healing, the spiritual gift to be offered is the man himself.

VERS. 7.-- I will come and heal him. And the centurion, making answer, said, etc. Great humility, etc., as far as he might be thought to go bodily because of a weakness of strength.

VERS. 8.-- I am not worthy. Because of the consciousness 113Bof his Gentile life, he fears rather to be burdened by his presence than to be helped, for although he was endowed with faith, he was not yet steeped in the sacraments. But what our infirmity does not even presume, grace knows how to give.

VERS. 9.-- For I also, etc. If I, a man, etc., as far as as Cornelius is called a just man and one that feareth God (Act. X).

VERS. 11.-- From the east and the west they shall come and shall sit down. Or the east means those who, being enlightened, straightway pass over; the west, those who bear sufferings even unto death. By these two quarters of the world, out of which the Gentiles were to be called, they are designated.

VERS. 12.-- But the children of the kingdom shall be cast out. Because in this world they enjoy the benefits of God; according to that word: He raineth upon the just and the unjust. Now they are seen in 113Cthe face of the Lord, but then not at all. Into the exterior darkness. Because they shall be driven forth from the Lord, etc., as far as he repents of what he obstinately did amiss. Weeping. By this is shown the greatness of the torments. By the weeping, the torment of the soul; by the gnashing of teeth, that of the body.

VERS. 14.-- And when he was come, etc. When this was done Matthew has not expressed, etc., as far as to suggest to his memory the things to be narrated. And sick of a fever. Fever is said in Greek, heat in Latin: and if at any time this affliction makes one tremble, it belongs to human nature.

VERS. 15.-- And he touched, etc. Luke says that they besought him, etc., as far as as Who can understand sins: from my secret sins cleanse me (Psal. VIII). And she arose, etc. 113DIt is natural for the fevered to grow faint when health begins, etc., as far as she ministers in the spirit.

VERS. 16.-- And when evening was come, etc. The setting of the sun is the death of Christ, through which he healed more demoniacs than, living before in the flesh, he had healed sick men.

VERS. 18.-- And Jesus seeing. It is manifest that it is another day, etc., as far as that we may pass over the bitter waves of the world.

VERS. 19.-- And there came. That Matthew says this was done when he bade them go across the strait: but Luke as they walked in the way: is not contrary: because they walked in the way that they might come to the strait. Master. As though he were one of many. And he himself was a man of letters, not a spiritual hearer. Master. He did not say 114ALord, but Master: reckoning him one among many masters.

VERS. 20.-- The foxes have holes. As if he should say: Why me, for the gains of the world, etc., as far as there is no place for the divinity of Christ. But the Son of man hath not, etc. Although men call him the Son of God, he himself calls himself the Son of man, for humility's sake.

VERS. 21.-- And another. Note that this man was called by Jesus. Lord. He who thrust himself forward calls him Master, like the puffed-up Pharisee, the other, who was received, humbly calls him Lord, like the publican.

VERS. 22.-- Their dead. Those who went out in fault, as they themselves live with fault. To bury the dead is to cherish sinners in their sin.

114BVERS. 23.-- And when he went up. He who did great things on land passes over to the sea, that he may do them there also, and appear Lord of land and sea.

VERS. 24.-- Behold a commotion. The tempest did not arise of itself, but at his command: that the disciples might fear the more and entreat him: and that to those entreating he might show his power. He was asleep. The Lord sleeps in us, that is, faith in the Lord, when we act more negligently, and therefore comes the tempest. But if faith rises again, he commands the winds and the waves.

VERS. 25. They awaked him. First, Jesus must be roused in tribulations by prayers, that afterwards, rising unto rebuke, he may command the winds and the sea, and there may be a great calm.

VERS. 26.-- O ye of little faith, etc. If you have known him powerful 114Con land, why do you not believe him so on the sea also? He who believes little is reproved: he who believes nothing is despised. He commanded, etc. That which is commanded feels him who commands. Note that every creature feels its Creator. The things which are insensible with us are to him things that feel.

VERS. 27.-- What manner of man is this, etc. (ORIG.) What manner of man is this, greater than Moses, etc., as far as they have no hearing, they obey.

VERS. 28. Into the country of the Gerazenes. Geraza is a city of Arabia, etc., as far as visited by Christ through his preachers. Two having demons. Mark and Luke say one, etc., as far as they showed the foulness of their bodies. Exceeding fierce. Mark says that no one now [could bind] the demoniac, etc., as far as they assail any who would pass.

VERS. 29.-- Jesus, Son of God. (HILAR.) They are to be believed rather to have suspected 114Dthan to have known. (AMBR.) Jesus, Son of God. He became known to them just so far as he willed, etc., as far as what they saw in him, they did not believe. Before the time. For they knew that there would be for them at the judgment of God, etc., as far as for there were two thousand, as Mark says. To torment us. The presence of Christ is a torture to them; or, they are tormented while they are cast out of the bodies in whose torture they took delight, just as the presence of God is the refreshment of the angels.

VERS. 31.-- Send us. Note that they can far less harm men by their own power, who cannot do so even to beasts. Unless a man live after the manner of a swine, the devil receives no power over him, save perhaps for his testing.

VERS. 32.-- And behold with great violence. [Vulg. Mt 8:32 et ecce impetu abiit totus grex, without magno] Man is worthier than beasts, 115Asince for the salvation of two men two thousand swine perished. The people of the Gentiles being set free, unbelievers practise their evil rites in hidden places: sunk in blind and deep curiosity.

VERS. 33.-- And the keepers fled. Because certain leaders even of the impious, though they flee the faith of Christ, yet do not cease to proclaim his power in wonder.

VERS. 34.-- Went out to meet him. (BED.) Those coming forth signify certain men bound by habit, who indeed admire and venerate the law of Christ, but are unwilling to suffer it: whence they beg him to pass on. And they found the man (as Luke says) clothed and in his right mind, sitting at the feet of Jesus (Luc. VIII). They besought him. They beseech him not out of pride, out of humility judging themselves unworthy, as Peter did, 115Band the widow in the case of Elias. They feared lest the presence of his holiness should destroy them because of their sins, as it had destroyed the swine.

CAPUT IX.

VERS. 1.-- In civitatem. Ne putes hanc esse Nazareth, etc., usque ad Capharnaum quasi metropolis.

VERS. 2.-- Offerebant. Doctores sunt, qui sublevant et offerunt Christo monendo et intercedendo. Fidem. Quantum valet apud Deum fides propria, apud quem sic valuit aliena, ut intus et extra sanaret hominem. Confide, fili, remittuntur tibi. Lucas dicit: Homo, etc., usque ad tollens causam morbi, post curat corpus.

VERS. 5.-- Quid est. Nota infirmitates aliquando 115Ccontingere propter peccata, etc., usque ad unde: Duplici contritione conteret eos Dominus. (BED.) Quid est facilius, sed quoniam hoc spirituale non creditis, probetur signo visibili quod non minoris constat esse potentiae, ut in Filio hominis latentem cognoscatis potentiam majestatis, quae potest dimittere peccata ut Deus.

VERS. 6.-- Tunc ait paralytico. Ecce per remissionem poenae, dimissionem culpae intellige. Surge. Surgere, est animam a carnalibus desideriis abstrahere. Lectum tollere, est carnem a terrenis desideriis ad voluntatem spiritus attollere. Domum ire, est ad paradisum redire, vel ad internam sui custodiam, ne iterum peccet. Tolle lectum tuum et vade in domum tuam. Ut quod fuit testimonium infirmitatis, 115Dsit probatio sanitatis.

VERS. 7.-- Et surrexit. Magna virtus, ubi sine mora imperium salus comitatur. Unde merito, qui aderant relictis blasphemiis stupentes, ad laudem convertuntur tantae majestatis.

VERS. 9.-- Vidit hominem. Hic probabilius videtur, quod praetermissa memoret, cum duodecim elegit ante sermonem. In telonio. Τέλος Graece, Latine vectigal. Matthaeus Hebraice, Latine donatus, qui tantum munus gratiae accepit: Levi, additus vel assumptus, quia divina electio eum assumpsit, et numero apostolorum addidit. Matthaeum. Caeteri Evangelistae propter verecundiam, etc., usque ad sermo supradictus fuit. Surgens. (BED.) Lucas plenius dicit: 116Arelictis omnibus, secutus est eum tam corpore quam mente. Non levitate ductus, sed veritate operum, et divina potentia intus operante tractus, perfecta obedientia, et perfecta abrenuntiatio, et lucra reliquit, cupidus sequendi Christum.

VERS. 10.-- In domo. Non dicit cujus, sed Lucas exprimit, etc., usque ad conversione poenitentium ostenditur. Ecce multi publicani. Bonum praesagium: jam ejus exemplo multi convertuntur. Jam post se multos trahit, Apostolus et doctor gentium futurus. (HIER.) Publicanis et peccatoribus. Tertullianus dicit hos fuisse ethnicos, etc., usque ad praecepit: In viam gentium ne abieritis. Peccatores venientes discumbebant. Poenitentes, exemplo Matthaei, veniam sperantes: unde Marcus dicit hic: quia multi sequebantur 116Beum (Marc. II).

VERS. 11.-- Quare cum publicanis. Omnis quaestio, etc., usque ad et omnia quae gessit, saluti serviunt.

VERS. 12.-- At Jesus audiens, ait. Et susceptos poenitentes amplius in fide confortat, et superbos ad humilitatis et pietatis gratiam provocat. Non est opus valentibus medicus, sed male habentibus. Medicus est Christus, etc., usque ad Dei verbum recipiunt, esurire non possunt.

VERS. 13.-- Euntes autem dicite. Tumentibus de falsa justitia consilium correctionis dat, etc., usque ad ove, et drachma, et duobus filiis. Non enim veni vocure justos. Nonne Nathanael, et Petrum, et Andream, etc., usque ad jam non justi sibi videntur.

116CVERS. 14.-- Discipuli autem tui non jejunant. Joannes, Marcus et Lucas, etc., usque ad gratiam et potestatem intelligas.

VERS. 15.-- Nunquid possunt. Quasi dicat: Facturi estis, ut lugentes, etc., usque ad sed ab omnibus terrenis. Lugere quandiu cum illis est sponsus. Hoc verbo exprimit illud jejunium, etc., usque ad postea jejunabunt, non lugebunt. Et tunc jejunabunt. Luctus de absentia sponsi, nec ante incarnationem defuit in patribus, nec post passionem, nisi quandiu in carne cum discipulis conversatus est. Jejunabunt. A mundi amore, non solum a cibis. Spirituale enim jejunium cum sit pars vitae novae, nondum eis convenit, qui per alia dediti sunt veteri vitae.

VERS. 16.-- Nemo autem. Quasi dicat: Carnales 116Dnondum fide passionis et resurrectionis solidati, etc., usque ad ipsi vetustati non convenit. Tollit enim. Id est perfectio novae vitae quae una est dividitur, et veteri fides quam habet aufertur, cui perfectio novae debebatur.

VERS. 17.-- Neque mittunt. Eosdem discipulos veteribus utribus comparat, etc., usque ad in novitate sensus intus reformamur.

VERS. 18.-- Ecce princeps unus accessit. Marcus et Lucas dicunt archisynagogum, etc., usque ad coepit remedium sperare et quaerere.

VERS. 20.-- Mulier quae. Salutem Judaeis paratam, spe certa praeripuit gentilitas. Duodecim annis. Duodecim annis fluxit, sicut et illa filia creverat 117Aduodenis: quia ex quo Synagoga coepit Deo famulari, coepit gentilitas idololatria et peccato pollui. Accessit. Credidit, dixit, quia his tribus fide et opere omnis salus acquiritur.

VERS. 21.-- Si tetigero. Fimbriam, non vestem. Quia non vidit in carne Dominum, sed suscepit per apostolos incarnationis verbum. Beatus qui vel extremam partem verbi fide tangit.

VERS. 22.-- Confide, filia. Credidit utique quae petiit, sed inculcat robur et perseverantiam in fide.

VERS. 23.-- Vidisset. Tibicines sunt carmen lugubre canantes. Hi sunt magistri moerore suo subditos demulcentes, et turbam Judaeorum adversus doctrinam Christi calcitrantium. Et turbam. Quia Synagoga laetitiam Dominicae inhabitationis, etc., 117Busque ad paruit in susceptione.

VERS. 24.-- Non est. Vobis mortua est. Deo autem qui suscitare potest, dormit, tam in anima quam corpore.
